Hi, thank you so much for having me. I really appreciate you taking the time. 

Um, I'm here because... well, it's a little embarrassing to admit, and it's probably just me being a little stressed or disorganized lately, but my mind just keeps going blank at the worst possible times. I work as a flagger, and lately I'll just be looking at the road-closure forms, or even just holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just... I completely blank. I can't remember the steps for what I'm supposed to do, even though it's a routine I've done for years. 

I hope it's okay that I came in for that? I just wanted to make sure I'm doing the right thing by getting it checked out, since I don't want to mess up at work. Does that make sense?

Well, um... I suppose that's actually the main reason I'm here, to be completely honest. I didn't want to make a big fuss about it, but... yes, making decisions and just figuring things out has gotten really hard lately.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, I don't think so. I mean, I might lose my train of thought sometimes, but I think that happens to everyone, right? I haven't really noticed any big problems with finding the right words or having to describe things instead of just saying what they are.

I'm a good team player. So, it's just a random little fuzzy thing, not because I'm nervous about being watched. Does that make sense? I hope that's helpful for your notes.\n\nDoctor: When one of these blank moments happens, about how long does it usually last before your mind clears again—just a few seconds, or does it stretch into minutes?\n\nPatient: Um, how long does it last? Well, um... I guess I have been feeling a little bit down lately. I mean, I really don't want to complain, because I know so many people have it worse, and I try to stay positive. I haven't hit my head or been sick or anything like that. I'm actually very healthy! \n\nI haven't been to a doctor for any new problems or been told I have diabetes or... or thyroid issues, or anything scary like that. I try to take really good care of myself so I can be reliable for work. I don't want you to think there's something majorly wrong with me physically, because there really isn't. I'm just a healthy person who happens to have a few little fuzzy moments at work. \n\nI hope that's a relief to hear? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I'm a very good sleeper, usually. \n\nAnd as for alcohol or... or drugs, oh no, I don't do anything like that. I might have a tiny glass of wine at a holiday dinner, maybe, but that's really it. I definitely don't use any recreational drugs. I know how important it is to be sharp when you're directing traffic, so I would never do anything to jeopardize that. I'm very responsible that way!\n\nI try to be a good patient and a good employee, you know? I hope that's the right answer. I just want to reassure you that I'm taking good care of myself. Oh, it's just a few seconds! Really, it's very brief. It's not like I'm standing there for minutes on end or anything. \n\nIt's just... well, it's actually why I came in, to be honest. My mind just keeps going blank at the worst times. Like, I'll look at the road-closure forms I need to fill out, or even just holding my stop-slow paddle, and for just a few seconds, I suddenly cannot remember the steps I've done for years. ========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.9"], "note": "The clear timeline showing mood symptoms (depressed mood, guilt, anorexia, anhedonia, low energy) preceding the cognitive/memory complaints by over a year strongly supports a diagnosis of major depressive disorder with secondary cognitive impairment (pseudodementia) rather than a primary neurocognitive disorder.
